<meta>This page continues the discussion on planetary positions and their influence on a person's destiny, specifically focusing on the strength gained through house placement.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>JATAKACHUNDRIKA <note>Moonlight of Natal Astrology</note></header>

Production depends upon the strength of the planets who produce them. Planets are classed into good and evil and a distinction is made between them when they own quadrants <note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses in a birth chart</note>. Owning, it must be remembered, is different from occupying a house.

The author says planets which occupy quadrants produce better results and this general statement is corroborated by other authors in astrology. Planets occupying trines <note>The 1st, 5th, and 9th houses</note> are productive of good results, and those which are exalted are of course, the best we can have. Exaltation implies the possession of the highest power to do good. Such planets when they are in conjunction with friendly planets, produce better results. He does not distinctly enumerate the cases where they produce better results. He simply says *better results. Better than what?* must be explained by the student as he progresses in his knowledge of astrology. Planets in <term>kandras</term> <note>Sanskrit term for quadrants or angular houses</note> are said to produce better results. This is supported by Badarayana (Vyasa) <note>A legendary sage often identified with the author of the Mahabharata and various astrological treatises</note>. But it is to be accepted in my humble opinion with great deference to the learned writers with certain modifications. Sani <note>Saturn</note> occupying the tenth, Kuja <note>Mars</note> occupying the 7th, Ravi <note>The Sun</note> occupying the 4th, and Khsinachundra <note>The waning or New Moon</note> occupying the first are no doubt planets who occupy <term>Kendras</term>. I humbly submit that the combination above given certainly produces ill-health and difficulties. Perhaps Badarayana means by the expression <note>original: "Kandhra Sthathi Balasyuah"</note> "those staying in quadrants are strong" that good planets occupying quadrants produce prosperity, making exception in the case of Saturn in the 7th, Mars and the Sun in the 10th.

---

->If a planet is in a friendly sign, or if it is aspected by the Minister (Jupiter),<-
->or if it is with a friend and powerful, the man will become equal to a king. 75.<-

### STANZA—75.

Planets which occupy friendly houses, which are in conjunction with friendly planets and which are powerful, when aspected by Guru <note>Jupiter, the "Teacher" or "Minister" of the gods</note>, make a man attain to a position which may be equal to that of a king.

<summary>This page discusses the nuances of planetary strength based on their placement in quadrants and trines, debating the traditional views of the sage Badarayana. It concludes with a verse stating that planets in friendly signs aspected by Jupiter can elevate a person to royal status.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, quadrants, trines, exaltation, Saturn, Mars, Sun, Jupiter, Badarayana, planetary aspects, royal status</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the concluding commentary on the Jatakachundrika, focusing on the final stanzas and the general principles of planetary periods.</meta>

# JATAKACHUNDRIKA.

### NOTES.

Manuscripts vary considerably regarding the final few stanzas that complete this work. I will not burden the reader with various readings or attempt to identify which specific stanza was likely written by the original author. The placement of planets in friendly houses is beneficial, as is their conjunction with friendly planets. It is noted elsewhere that planets must otherwise be powerful to be effective. They possess several sources of strength <note>referring to Shadbala, or the six-fold system of planetary potency</note>, and these sources must also be favorable. When the aspect of the most auspicious planet, <term>Guru</term> <note>Jupiter, the "Great Teacher" and most benevolent planet in Vedic astrology</note>, is added to these indicators of power, there is no doubt that their influence will elevate a man to the status of a king. This refers specifically to power and position; happiness itself may not depend on the possession of these two. In one version, the author is credited with only 79 stanzas, while another increases the number to 81. This discrepancy is likely due to omissions or intentional alterations by vain men into whose hands the early manuscripts fell. I will provide the meaning of the 79 stanzas and thus complete the work.

---

->Planets, according to their designation as auspicious or inauspicious,<-
->grant results only as good in their own major and sub-periods. || 76 ||<-
<note>original: "शुभपापात्मकतया निर्दिष्टेभ्य फलं ग्रहाः । प्रदिशन्ति शुभा एव स्वदशासु स्वभुक्तिषु ॥ ७६ ॥"</note>

### STANZA. 76.

Planets, by becoming good or evil and being bound by well-defined principles, produce good or evil in their own <term>Dasas</term> <note>Major planetary periods in a person's life</note> and <term>Bhukties</term> <note>Sub-periods within a Dasa</note>.

### NOTES.

This is again vaguely expressed. The commentaries are conflicting, and the meaning must be carefully discerned. The main idea seems to be that planets, whether naturally good or evil, produce favorable results or the reverse during their own specific periods and sub-periods. Planets are further subject to definite principles of procedure; they cannot deviate from their declared "policy," whatever that may be. It is doubtful whether a good planet produces good in its own sub-period when

<summary>The author discusses the discrepancies in manuscript versions of the Jatakachundrika and explains Stanza 76 regarding how planets manifest their good or evil effects during their specific time-periods (Dasas and Bhukties).</summary>

<keywords>Jatakachundrika, Guru, Jupiter, Dasa, Bhukti, planetary periods, astrology, manuscript variations, Stanza 76</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on planetary periods (Dasas) and concludes with a philosophical reflection on the limitations of astrological prediction.</meta>

# JATAKACHUNDRIKA

The period <note>Dasa: a major planetary period in Hindu astrology</note> of a planet begins, and the same doubt may be expressed regarding evil planets. The author has not stated anywhere that the sub-period of a planet within its own major period—whether that planet is inherently good or bad—produces favorable results. However, he makes a general statement here which may be explained more satisfactorily through other principles found in the astrological sciences.

---

->The wise indicate the results of fate by the movement of the planets;<-
->But who, except the Creator Brahma himself, can speak to the exact distinctions of the future? <note>original: "Phalani grahacharena suchayanti manishinah; Ko vakta taratamyasya tam ekam vedhasam vina."</note> || 77 ||<-

### STANZA 77.

The learned scholars of astrology suggest the outcomes of planetary influences based on the movements of the celestial bodies; but excepting the great Creator <term>Brahma</term> <note>The Hindu god of creation, considered the source of all knowledge</note> himself, no one can definitely speak to the minute details which can be predicted from the astrological sciences.

### NOTES.

The learned have found, either by inspiration or by careful and close study, that the influences of the planets are calculable and can therefore be predicted. The author then, I believe, simply points out the difficulties inherent in mastering a science such as this. By its very nature, it is a highly complex field. The influences of only nine planets are taken into consideration, and from these nine planets and the twelve <term>zodiacal</term> signs, the entire phenomena of the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms must be carefully analyzed. 

The difficulties that lie in the way of predicting results are therefore too numerous to be easily overcome by ordinary minds. The author suggests that although learned <term>Pundits</term> <note>Scholars or teachers, particularly those skilled in Sanskrit and traditional law or science</note> in astrology can indicate good and evil results by watching planetary revolutions and counter-revolutions, they will not be able to grasp every detail. This is a common form of humility among Sanskrit authors, offered to show the vastness of the "sea of knowledge" that remains unexplored. The great Brahma possesses knowledge of the entire world, and He alone knows the details of the astrological and other sciences perfectly. He is called the <term>Sarvagana</term> <note>The All-Knowing or Omniscient</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the technical discussion on planetary sub-periods and offers a humble acknowledgment that while astrology can suggest general outcomes, only the Creator possesses perfect knowledge of the future.</summary>
<keywords>Dasa, Brahma, Pundits, zodiac, Sarvagana, astrological prediction, Jatakachundrika, planetary movements</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page concludes the primary text of the Jataka Chundrika and begins the author's concluding historical and biographical commentary.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>THE MOONLIGHT OF ASTROLOGY.</header>

->Born as the son of the performer of sacrifices named Yagnanarayana,<-
->The Moonlight of Astrology was composed by the venerable Venkateshwara. || 78 ||<-
<note>original: "यज्ञनारायणाभिख्या यायजूक तनूभव । श्रीवेङ्कटेश्वराय्येन कृता जातकचन्द्रिका ॥ ७८ ॥" This is the concluding colophon of the work.</note>

### STANZA-78.

This work, *The Moonlight of Astrology* <note>original: "Jataka Chundrika"</note>, has been written by the learned scholar Venkateshwara Sastri <note>original: "Venkatasa Sastrulu"</note>, son of the ritualist Yagnanarayana <term>Dikshit; a title for one who has performed traditional Vedic sacrifices</term>.

### NOTES.

The father of the author seems to have been a *Dikshit* or one who had performed various <term>Yagnams; sacred Vedic fire sacrifices</term>. The nobility of the author’s birth is therefore placed beyond any doubt. The author calls himself a learned scholar, and he could not have been otherwise. In the course of 75 stanzas, he has tried to lead the astrological student to the doors of future predictions, and he tells him there that by diligence he would find admission into the sacred precincts of the unquestionable truths contained in Astrology.

The age of the work cannot be so easily marked. The free use of the names of the shadow planets <term>Rahu; the North Node of the Moon</term> and <term>Ketu; the South Node of the Moon</term> show clearly that the work must have been more recent than that of the *Great Compendium of Nativity* <note>original: "Brihat Jataka"</note> by the teacher Varahamihira, who flourished during the time of King Vikramaditya and who was one of the "nine gems" <note>the legendary group of nine scholars</note> which graced that illustrious monarch's reign. The age of Vikramaditya is roughly stated to be about 2,000 years ago. I do not even think that *The Moonlight of Astrology* is even so old as that. It must have been written pretty recently, perhaps in the 12th or 13th centuries A.D., when Astrology was prevalent all over India. The name sounds like a South Indian name, and it is difficult to identify his birthplace and that of his education. I shall hereafter commence to collect information regarding the history of the various astrological writers whose works have prominently appeared before the public and about whom little information has reached them. It may not be an easy task, but any correct details which may come—

<summary>This page identifies the author of the Jataka Chundrika as Venkateshwara Sastri and provides a historical analysis of the text's likely origins in 12th or 13th-century South India.</summary>
<keywords>Venkateshwara Sastri, Yagnanarayana Dikshit, Jataka Chundrika, Varahamihira, Vikramaditya, Rahu, Ketu, South Indian Astrology, dating of manuscripts</keywords>

to my knowledge in the course of my travels or my investigations will be published for the benefit of the public, so that these details may well be criticised by the learned before they are accepted as so many truths.
